Milan entered the international break with a much-needed victory against Genoa, but it wasn’t the easiest result of the season. After a disappointing defeat to Ferencvaros – which extended the midweek run of defeats – they couldn’t afford to drop points, and they succeeded, even if it wasn’t without flaw.

With two goals inside 20 minutes, you’d be forgiven for expecting Milan to win by more than a couple. However, that was the end of their goalscoring for the afternoon and although Genoa pulled one back, they didn’t grab more than a consolation goal.

Speaking to Sky Sport – in the new series ‘Radici’ – De Rossi looked back on the defeat to the Rossoneri, as well as a comment that stuck with him from Diego Moreira, as MilanPress have relayed today.

We indeed lost against Milan, but we lost ‘well.’ We didn’t deserve it. Three or four weeks ago, the latest results seemed like a pipe dream. I thank the players, staff, and my club for giving me faith and patience at a time when other clubs wouldn’t have had it.

“They remembered how good we did last year. I’m happy to have emerged from a difficult period and to be Genoa’s coach; I’m really happy there.”

“I’m passionate about the fans and the supporters. I’m part of a club that, in terms of fan base, is among the best in Europe. I’m passionate; I don’t punch a clock. I spend two hours with the stockman or the waitress with the same enthusiasm I spend time with the team captain. Experiencing it like that, you realise that Genoa is a great team.

“Genoa is full of Genoese fans who remind you every second how much they love their club, the pride of being the top club in Italy. The architecture of the stadium and the warmth of the fans… I’m not saying this to be a sycophant, but on Sunday Moreira came to me and said, ‘I’ve never seen anything like this here in Italy.’ It’s a reality that even our opponents notice.”

#2157. West Ham, Millwall and Norwich City among clubs interested in League Two loan star

Clubs up and down the country will be preparing for the January transfer window, and among those likely to be active are Championship trio West Ham, Millwall and Norwich City. It has been said that WHUFC boss Scott Parker is prioritising a new striker and a left-back, but TEAMtalk states the club have eyes on centre-back Jack Taylor.

It is reported that the Hammers are alongside the Canaries and arch-rivals Millwall in tracking Taylor, who has thrived on loan at Exeter City from Stevenage this season. A host of clubs scouted the 21-year-old in action against Huddersfield Town earlier this month – a game in which he scored.

Taylor has Premier League admirers too though, with Brighton & Hove Albion, Everton, Brentford, Crystal Palace and Fulham taking an interest. Clubs are considering winter moves with the possibility of keeping him on loan at Exeter City for the remainder of the season.

Taylor, born in Bexhill-on-Sea in 2005, has risen through non-league to the EFL. He started out in the Eastbourne Borough setup before moving to Sutton United, and a string of loans in the lower leagues ultimately earned him a first-team spot at Gander Green Lane.

Stevenage came calling in January 2026, but limited opportunities prompted his summer loan to Exeter City. The 21-year-old has had to wait for his chance, but Taylor has now started the last eight League Two games on the trot. The Grecians have won five of those and kept five clean sheets in the process.

Taylor’s per 90 stats show just how effective he has been since breaking into Matt Taylor’s side. And, it is little wonder that sides like West Ham, Millwall, Norwich City and Premier League clubs are taking note.

No League Two centre-back betters Taylor’s 17.27 defensive contributions per 90, and he ranks top for his 79.2% duel win rate as well. He also averages 6.24 aerial duel wins, 11.51 clearances and 2.69 tackles per game (all stats via FotMob).

It isn’t often that young players rise through non-league and reach high levels anymore, such is the level of competition throughout the English pyramid. So, for Taylor to emerge as he has and draw interest from top clubs speaks volumes about his potential.

However, given his steep trajectory and age, admirers like West Ham, Millwall and Norwich City will have to be patient with his development. Taylor could yet benefit from seeing out a full season in League Two or playing some League One football before moving to the Championship – or even the Premier League.

Unfortunately for Exeter City, they are passengers when it comes to a decision over Taylor’s future. The call will be Stevenage’s to make, and they may well be inclined to cash in if the opportunity arises amid interest from WHUFC and more.

#2156. GdS: Linear structure and a strong work force – what Krösche is likely to implement at Milan

AC Milan are expected to hire Markus Krösche in the next few weeks, and a report has looked a little more at his work today.

Between Eintracht Frankfurt and Milan, there are plenty of differences. From the finances available to how they’re spent, even to the way in which the structure of the club ticks over. So, Krösche will have to learn with the Rossoneri.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

However, it won’t be a one way street, with the Diavolo needing to learn plenty too.

As Gazzetta dello Sport writes, Krösche methods are very linear; it’s about arriving and leaving at the right time, having the knowledge to enter and the courage to step away when the time is right.

His belief is that the club will always hold more weight than the manager, which has been shown in the past few months by Gerry Cardinale and there is no shortage of exciting signings on his CV.

What is also worth remembering is that he’s not one to step away from his methods. In 2022, he was given a chance to sign Cristiano Ronaldo. However, it didn’t make sense for him or Frankfurt, so they never made their move.

If it happens his way, Timmo Hardung will arrive alongside the German. The pair met at RB Leipzig and their relationship has only grown since then and Krösche eventually led a team of 70 people at Frankfurt. In other words, he won’t be out of place at the top of the Milan football organisation.

Gazzetta even suggests that it’s ‘no secret’ that his desire is to move to Italy to work under Krösche once again. Given their successes together, it makes sense why all parties are hoping to keep that relationship under the same roof.

#2154. Derby County will hope influential star can build on Bolton Wanderers display and hit form after a tough season: Opinion

Derby County remain rooted in the Championship relegation zone after a tough season to date, but they enjoyed a much-needed win over Bolton Wanderers before the break. Not only did the 3-1 triumph mark the end of a 10-game winless streak for DCFC, but it might just have kept John Eustace in his job.

Ryan Nyambe made headlines for scoring his first career goal in the win, while Jack Robinson and Rhian Brewster also got on the scoresheet. They were among the standout players against the Trotters, while Mo Fuseini and Jacob Widell Zetterstrom also impressed.

Carlton Morris was another man to play a central role in the win. The Rams striker has come under fire amid his poor form at the start of the 2026/27 season, but after a much-improved display against Bolton Wanderers, Morris will be eager to kick on and get back to his dangerous best in front of goal.

Although some have overstepped the mark, criticism of Morris has been justified this term. He hasn’t been helped by a Derby County side desperately lacking in creative spark and quality in possession, but one goal in 12 games across all competitions marks a big drop-off from last season.

His sole goal has come from only 2.38 xG in the Championship too, so not only has he struggled to convert his opportunities, he isn’t getting many high-quality chances either (FotMob).

Morris’ wait for a first Championship goal since August continued against Bolton Wanderers, but the all-round play of the 30-year-old striker was far better. It was his header across the box that Robinson scored the second goal from. And, after he held up the ball well, his cross was flicked onto Nyambe by Fuseini for the equaliser.

The DCFC man created three chances in total and won 11 aerial duels (FotMob). He was tireless in attack for Eustace as they fought to secure a key win, and it showed why the boss has retained faith in Morris.

There have been criticisms about his mobility from supporters. But, in Morris, Eustace has a powerful outlet at the top of the pitch who will run and battle for his team. He leads from the front, and if he can rediscover his goalscoring touch, he can play a key role in turning around the Rams’ fortunes.

Morris’ display against Bolton Wanderers showed how he brings other players into the game and offered a timely reminder of how his aerial ability and physical traits can be used with better service.

But, ultimately, Morris needs to start scoring to really silence doubters and keep his place. Lars-Jorgen Salvesen has found game time hard to come by but his two goals equate to 0.75 goals per 90. It has raised fair arguments that the Norwegian should be given a starting chance over Morris.

Morris scored 12 goals in 31 games last season and he was excellent before injury ended his campaign early. His Bolton Wanderers performance was a much-needed reminder of just how influential he can be, and it can be a catalyst for the striker as he looks to inspire DCFC’s rise away from the Championship relegation zone.

#2152. Stadi italiani per Euro2032, Uva: "C'è senso di serietà. Ecco quando devono essere pronti"

Verso EURO 2032 - in condivisione con la Turchia - qual è il punto degli stadi in Italia? L’obiettivo è arrivare alla scelta degli impianti che ospiteranno la competizione e ad aggiornare sulla situazione è stato Michele Uva, UEFA Executive Director di Euro 2032 per l’Italia, intervenuto durante l’evento "RCS Sport Industry Talk" a Milano.

"Il programma è in linea per la scelta delle sedi che ospiteranno l’Europeo. C’è un senso di serietà, perché c’era un senso di sfiducia anche dei media, ma ricordo che gli stadi devono essere pronti nel 2031", ha dichiarato, riguardo ai lavori e ai progetti infrastrutturali in vista del torneo. "L’Europeo deve essere un acceleratore sportivo, che cambierà l’approccio alla gara ma anche allo sport in generale, un acceleratore anche sociale", le dichiarazioni riprese da Calcio e Finanza.

EURO 2032 si terrà in Italia e Turchia, per la 19ª edizione degli Europei - gli ultimi disputati in Germania. Saranno cinque città per ciascun Paese a ospitare le partite del torneo. Il 30 settembre 2026 la FIGC ha inviato alla UEFA i dossier delle sedi ritenute idonee a proseguire il percorso di candidatura per ospitare le partite di EURO 2032. Al momento in Italia ci sono 13 città e 14 stadi in corsa: Bari, Salerno, Torino, Bologna, Palermo, Milano, Cagliari, Cagliari, Firenze, Napoli, Genova, Lecce, Roma (con lo Stadio Olimpico e il nuovo stadio di Pietralata), oltre che Verona. Adesso starà all'organo di Nyon verificare le dovute verifiche degli impianti per ospitare la competizione (circa 120 requisiti operativi), tra cui compatibilità infrastrutturale, accessibilità, sostenibilità, servizi per i tifosi, sicurezza, hospitality e servizi per media e broadcaster. A quel punto verranno indicate le cinque sedi italiane definitive per ospitare le gare di EURO 2032.

#2151. 'He always wanted to play for Scotland'. Alex McLeish recalls the moment the national team found their new talisman in Scott McTominay

By JOHN MCGARRY, SCOTTISH CHIEF SPORTS FEATURE WRITER

Published: 17:30 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 18:02 EST, 11 November 2026

While Alex McLeish’s second stint as Scotland manager clearly did not go to plan, the compensations from that period proved to be significant for the national team.

Without a struggling side earning a play-off spot in the Nations League, a famous night in Serbia under Steve Clarke would never have been possible.

For all we know, the years in international exile may have rolled on. The ongoing improvement which led to participation at the World Cup this summer might never have happened.

McLeish’s greatest legacy, however, was surely to persuade Scott McTominay to pull on a dark blue shirt after he travelled to Carrington in 2018.

Alerted to the Lancaster-born player’s eligibility by then Manchester United manager Jose Mourinho, the man who won 77 caps for his country could have been forgiven for feeling that the coup he pulled off was just never meant to be.

‘The beast from the east,’ McLeish recalled with a grin.

Scott McTominay (left) and Alex McLeish in 2018 ahead of a friendly against Belgium

McLeish and McTominay together in a Nations League encounter against Albania in 2018

‘When I was forced off the train in Newcastle, they said “it’s not going any further, the weather’s knackered.”

‘So, I had to wait for a train down south. And I thought “at this point will I go home?”

‘And I thought, “no, I’ve come this far, so I’m going all the way”. Then I got to my hotel at five in the morning, the guy says, “oh, I thought you were a no-show, son.”

‘I said - “no - I’ve arrived and I’m needing a room.”

‘I didn’t go in for the breakfast as Mourinho said “just come in at lunchtime, get some food and we’ll have a chat.”

‘But he said he (McTominay) definitely wants to play for Scotland.

‘So, it was good to hear that from Jose.’

Mourinho liked what he saw the minute he set eyes on the player. It’s fair to say that not everyone at the club around that time was similarly enthused.

‘He was still just a kid, quite raw,’ McLeish recalled. ‘But he had the physique, speed and skill on the ball.

‘He was good in the air and also with the ball at his feet. It made it a no-brainer.

‘I’d gone to a couple of Man United games and they were just kind of breeding him into the system.

McTominay in training ahead of the Nations League clash against North Macedonia on Friday

‘Listen, anyone would have done it and brought him into the squad.

‘The only problem we faced was that England were in the frame as well. But when I said to Scott, “I’d love you to play for Scotland,” he said “That’s the only team I want to play for.”

‘And all of a sudden we had a brand new young superstar.’

Truth be told, it would be some time before Scotland supporters began to place McTominay in such a bracket.

He had his moments early on for his country, but his deployment as a defender – by both McLeish and Clarke – did him no favours.

It was only in the qualifiers to Euro 2024 that the futility of that approach was accepted.

McTominay was outstanding as a central midfielder in the qualifiers, his performance and two goals in the famous win against Spain topping the lot.

In little over a year, he’d be a Napoli player. And within a season, he’d become a Serie A winner and voted Italy’s best player.

Many Manchester United fans were outraged that the club had sold him for just £25.7million.

Even some England supporters voiced their displeasure about the player slipping through their hands.

‘They have such a massive pot to pick from in England,’ McLeish reflected.

‘But I’m sure that privately, in some of their chats behind the scenes, they will have said ‘we should have got McTominay.’

‘But he chose Scotland. He wanted to to come. He wasn’t saying anything about England at all.

‘And that was the best news I could have got in that era of rebuild.’

While Clarke rightly got the plaudits for taking the team to three tournaments, McLeish has taken a huge amount of pride from the part McTominay has played in it all.

‘It’s incredible how he’s grown and grown,’ he added.

‘In the first few games, he was just trying to feel his way into the international scene. But he gave everything from that first call-up.

Scott McTominay in action against Haiti in the 2026 World Cup

‘He’s played in just about every position in the team down the years. He was in the back four at one stage.

‘But he’s mainly operated in the middle of the field and he’s a real threat in the box and moving from middle to front.

‘The way he runs with the ball is just superb because he really knows what he’s doing.

‘He’s got massive confidence and I hope that a lot of young Scottish players coming into the game can take inspiration from what Scott has done.'

In a rare media conference this week, McTominay put his underwhelming displays at the World Cup in context.

Two days before the opener against Haiti in Boston, he underwent a controlled electric shock in hospital to restore a normal heart rhythm. Since then, he’s undergone a medical procedure to deal with the arrhythmia.

He returns to the Scotland squad a month after pulling on a Napoli shirt again against Frosinone.

With five goals in his past six matches, it feels like the player the Tartan Army grew to love is back with them.

Sebastien Pocognoli may soon feel he owes McLeish a word of thanks, too.

‘It’ll be a massive relief for the manager to get Scott back into the squad,’ he said.

‘You always want your best players available.

‘I remember back in my day, going to Kazakhstan when we had a lot of boys missing.

‘We were held in a bit of limbo. So, for Sebastien to get players into his team of that level will be massive for him. It could make a huge difference for Scotland.’

#2149. Barnsley vs Sheffield Wednesday predictions lodged as the Tykes ‘saddening’ decline continues

Barnsley come into Friday’s Yorkshire derby against Sheffield Wednesday in dire straits. The Tykes sit 23rd in the League One table with just nine points to their name after seven defeats in their last nine matches in the third tier.

Daniel Stendel‘s side had found some respite in domestic competition with wins over Aldershot Town and Oldham Athletic in the FA Cup and EFL Trophy respectively. However, their league woes continued with a 2-1 defeat away at fellow strugglers Wigan Athletic last Saturday.

As for Sheffield Wednesday, they claimed a valuable three points in Dave Challinor’s first League One game last weekend. Billy Mitchell, Davis Keillor-Dunn and Di’Shon Bernard scored on the way to an important 3-0 win over Plymouth Argyle, marking the Owls’ first league victory in four matches.

SWFC have to get back on track on the road though, having lost back-to-back games against Notts County and Doncaster Rovers by 2-0 scorelines as Henrik Pedersen‘s tenure came to an end. Wednesday now sit 5th, six points away from the automatic promotion spots.

“Barnsley and Sheffield Wednesday are in drastically contrasting positions. The Tykes are lingering in the League One relegation zone with continued discontent towards the ownership, while SWFC are hoping to challenge for promotion as they rebuild under ambitious leadership.

“Daniel Stendel marked an exciting summer appointment at Oakwell but he has been completely hung out to dry. The decline in quality of that squad is just saddening, and with 24 goals conceded this season, I have little faith that their defence can curtail the visitors’ quality this Friday.

“SWFC are finding their feet under Dave Challinor, but the 3-0 win over Plymouth Argyle marked an exciting result in his first league game. Davis Keillor-Dunn is in fine form and Di’Shon Bernard is a true Championship quality centre-back, and a big asset at this level.

“Perhaps the occasion can motivate Stendel’s young side and drive them to a surprise result. However, you can’t help but feel the visitors win in pretty comfortable fashion at Oakwell.”

“Barnsley are in serious danger of being relegated this season. Their form has been abysmal at home and with no clean sheets at Oakwell this term, they will be up against it on Friday.

“It’s a great opportunity for the Tykes to incite revenge on the Owls who beat them in the play-off final three years ago. However, the difference in squad talent is a lot greater this time around.

“Sheffield Wednesday have been terrific on the road and with an average of 1.67 points a game, promotion is a real possibility. However, two losses away from home in a row should give a glimmer of hope to Stendel’s side.

“I can’t see the hosts winning this one, though, and Challinor will be eager to continue his winning start as boss. I think SWFC will be far too strong on the night.”

#2148. Tare discusses new Milan direction, limitations in his role and ‘jealousy’: “Better late than never”

Igli Tare has spoken without holding anything back about his time at AC Milan, explaining his frustration with several things.

When Tare arrived, many Milan fans were happy to see someone with Serie A experience oversee the project and whilst his first season didn’t end how anyone had hoped, he wasn’t seen as a major issue.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

So, when it was announced that he was part of a complete overturning of the management, there was a bit of a surprise, even if it did feel inevitable at one point.

With the overhaul of changes in the summer, one does wonder what Tare would have done differently, especially with suggestions that his hands were tied in the mercato last season. Nevertheless,

Speaking on Sky Sport’s ‘Calcio Unplugged’, Igli Tare revealed all to Gianluca Di Marzio and Stefano Borghi in an in-depth interview about his time with the Diavolo, as Radio Rossonera has relayed.

“Today is the first time I’ve spoken out after all these months. The wound of not having reached the Champions League is still enormous, and since I arrived at Milan – also questioning myself with the type of contract I signed – the objective couldn’t be only the Champions League; otherwise I wouldn’t have understood where I was.”

“It was great because he was the one who convinced me. He had such a huge desire to join Milan. I wanted to video chat before going to see him because I couldn’t believe it. But after five minutes, I knew in my head that he was the player Milan really lacked.

“Not just because of his qualities, but as a man and as a figure that that team didn’t have. He never said no. A photo with a child, an autograph, a meeting, talking to someone… He never said no. These are the things that humility is key to in life.

“I’m proud to have brought a great champion to Milan. In my opinion, if he wants, he can play another two seasons without any problem. He never missed a training session. Sometimes we were the ones to stop him because he was always there, ready. Loving football at that age is truly an honour.”

Is it a characteristic of the champion or is it a generational issue? Today’s champions seem to have a different mentality:

“Today we live in the age of social media. At 16, you’re already a footballer who can earn millions of euros. Living this way, you have the problem of not only managing a player, who is still a child, but you have to manage the business they bring with them: family, agents, girlfriends…

“These are obstacles that didn’t exist back then. We were a generation that played in the streets. Back then, our parents beat us to get us to come home. Today, however, it’s to get them out of the house because they spend all day in front of computers.

“This aspect has been lost a bit, but it’s our fault. Education and discipline are the key to success.”

Fullkrug. He performed poorly in Milan, but in the Bundesliga he’s scoring a lot and is the top scorer. How do you explain that?

“It’s simple. He came to us after a tough time in England, with a lot of scepticism. And then, right away, he broke his toe. No one ever said he’d been having injections on the bone for two months just to train, let alone to play.

“I knew he was a centre forward that the team was missing; given his characteristics and his interpretation of the game, his qualities were unquestionable. Just two years before coming to Milan, he’d played in the Champions League final, playing a key role in a competition with Borussia Dortmund.

“There are situations where, for a player, even a very important one, it matters how you feel. If you feel appreciated, valued, taken into consideration… When you play for a club like Milan, the weight of the shirt is completely different than when you play for other clubs.

“You feel it from the first day you join. I had no doubt that he could be what he’s demonstrating now.”

“We tried to sign him back in June. We made a first attempt. In the final days of the transfer window, another important name we tried to sign was Akanji. The Champions League was crucial for both of them. They turned him down, not because of the club’s importance, but because he wasn’t playing in the Champions League.

“Then the opportunity arose again at the end of August for Rabiot. Defeat against Cremonese? No, we had already decided to bring him. It was just a matter of contract details.”

“It was difficult for us because we decided to change the formation five days before the transfer window closed. Switching from a 4-3-3 to a three-man defense meant we were having a really hard time finding a quality player in the last five days.

“We were a bit unlucky, first with the Akanji story and then with Liverpool’s Joe Gomez… Guehi’s move from Crystal Palace to Liverpool fell through, and at the last minute they—we already had the deal ready, we were just waiting for this to happen—made a step back. And to avoid being left without a central defender at the last minute, we signed Odogu as a promising player.”

On not being able to reach the Champions League and not being able to continue his work at Milan…

“This is the first time today, after all these months, that I’ve spoken about this… The wound of not reaching the Champions League is still enormous. I think that from the first day I arrived at Milan, even in the way I signed—I questioned myself with the type of contract.

“I said, ‘If I have to come only for the Champions League objective, then I don’t understand where I’ve come from.’ Milan has a different story. I had to go through a trial. The disappointment is that those who run the club didn’t understand how much time people needed, which is now being done, rightly so.

“That is to say, it takes several transfer sessions to get a competitive team ready to win. At the beginning, I felt the responsibility to put the pieces together immediately, because after the defeat in the Coppa Italia final the year before, there were some important signs.

“I didn’t know where to start. But I think we did an important job last summer, we sold for a lot and also signed 10 new players. Then you need time to integrate, learn new philosophies and create a cohesive group, hungry to win.

“Until March-April, we were a group with difficulties, but we were at the top of the table. Then in the last 10 matchdays, we had a mental breakdown, also due to other external dynamics, which I don’t want to discuss here, which had a significant impact.

“Internal or external dynamics? External to the team. But we also have some blame: myself first and foremost, along with Max [Allegri].

“We had to be a little more attentive to certain dynamics. We needed to be more present with the team to ultimately lead them to the final result. Isolation is the wrong word; I don’t believe in these things. I believe in teamwork; I believe in cohesion: from the club to the team.

“When this happens, you transmit it to the team. When these situations become possible, you could also see it in the team’s results, because the players are very attentive. Sometimes we think they have other interests, but they pay attention to everything that comes out. They follow everything very carefully and transmit it.

“Instilling serenity… How can you convey serenity? When, in difficult moments, you show that you believe in the project more than before. Sometimes I missed this feeling, a lack of something that could give us the final push.”

But despite this, did you think you could continue? Does this farewell after only a year feel like a stab in the back?

“Look, I would have asked for a round table where we could all sit down together and analyze everything. Then they could have just said ‘no, thanks’… But I respect the decision they made. I accept it with great regret, but I’ve never questioned it because there’s someone above me who has to decide, including for the fate of the club.

“And when they decide to take a different path, you have to accept it. I know things could have gone differently, even much better. I’ll always be a Milan fan, even after an experience like that.”

Was AC Milan a team that needed the constant presence of a manager or coach to avoid letting itself go as it later did?

“The feeling I had at the beginning was that this leadership was a bit lacking. The arrival of Modric—and another of our goals was also Granit Xhaka, Leverkusen’s captain—was precisely to bring real players into the group, with important characteristics and capable of leading others.

“Granit didn’t arrive; Luka arrived, who has a different way of being a leader: he does it in silence and with his attitude on the pitch. I had the opportunity to meet Mike Maignan: an extraordinary guy, a soul who cares deeply about the AC Milan shirt.

“Gabbia is another extraordinary guy; he lives for this club. Rabiot too… His experience spoke for itself. The great disappointment is that I expected something more in terms of responsibility and leadership from Pulisic: he’s a top-class player with unquestionable qualities. But in terms of performance, things happened that I had never experienced in my career…

“Like Gimenez: an extraordinary guy with numbers that spoke for themselves; for a year he failed to score a goal. Then it happened that Pulisic didn’t score for six months. He scored a goal. Then Leao didn’t score for four months.

“Situations that are hard to come by in football have happened… It can happen that you don’t score for a month, two months, but that long a period of time also affected the team’s final performance.

“Losing a match against Cagliari the way we did, 2-1, I think was the worst match of my life. The feeling and the disappointment I felt towards the fans in the stadium that night is something I will never forget for the rest of my life.”

If Cardinale had been the Cardinale he is today, the one who is also physically present, would you still be AC ​​Milan’s DS?

“I can’t answer that question; he has to answer it. I can say that I remember when the Jashari negotiations were going on… I had just landed in Rome and I was very pleased when he called me because he wanted to understand what was happening in that negotiation.

“I explained all the dynamics to him and I really liked that he said, ‘Anything you need, if you have to spend a little more, let me know because I’m here.’ Then during that process I never had the chance to talk to him, even intentionally.

“There were other people above me who I never wanted to bypass. I never had a direct relationship with him, and the possibility of a discussion would have helped everyone. Not me, but everyone. By putting all the problems and dynamics on the table, we could have resolved things much sooner. But I think it’s a new experience for Cardinale.

“He’s had experience in other sports in America. Now he’s done something important, taking responsibility and putting his face on the line. I would have preferred to have had that presence even when I was there because it would have been very useful. But for the Milan, better late than never.

“Now, for better or worse, he will make his own decisions and responsibilities. I think that for him, too, the best interests of Milan come first.”

Was Leao a problem or a resource in your mind?

“I knew him from the outside. When I had the chance to meet him in person, I thought he was an extraordinary guy. I truly loved him as a person, like everyone else. But he was the player from whom we all expected something more. I think football was no longer a priority for him. That’s the key to understanding him.”

“I think a bit about his life outside of football. Music, fashion, things… But I never doubted the sense of belonging he had towards Milan. You could sense that he loved playing for Milan and was suffering from not performing.

“He had many physical problems… First a calf, then a bit of pubalgia… He always played with his hands under control. I always tried to have individual conversations with him more than with any other Milan player.

“Every time I spoke to him, I left with a positive feeling. I sensed that he wanted to. But sometimes wanting isn’t enough.”

Was there a chance that Leao would leave during your summer too?

“A concrete offer never came for him. Regarding the other transfers, it’s not just Tare’s job. When I arrived – even at Lazio – I was a bit alone, but I tried to involve all the members of the club to give him the importance he needed to build a sense of belonging and responsibility. I think I did a very good job, together with Furlani, Ibra, and Moncada.

“We achieved significant sales figures, in a year when we weren’t even in the Champions League. Thanks to the work we did last summer, investments were made this year, with the money we had at the time. I really believe in teamwork.

“I said it even before the Coppa Italia match against Bari; I asked to speak to convey the importance of the work of the people within the club because it was right that way. Then afterwards, it’s human…

“They were coming off a bad year, when two cumbersome figures like Tare and Allegri arrive, and everyone was talking about Tare and Allegri, some disappointment was understandable. I understood the feeling they had, but I could… There was little to do about it.

“For me, it was a new path, a new opportunity. It was essential to work as a team and present ourselves as a real company.”

#2147. USA pulls out of U-17 World Cup just days before it starts… amid war in the Middle East and 'risk of armed conflict'

Published: 18:48 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 18:48 EST, 11 November 2026

The US Soccer Federation is withdrawing its team from the FIFA U-17 World Cup in Qatar amid growing security concern over the ongoing conflicts in the Middle East, according to multiple reports.

The American State Department currently has a travel advisory for the country at a Level 3, with guidance telling travelers to 'reconsider travel to Qatar due to risk of armed conflict.'

Athletes who were set to compete in the tournament - held from November 19 to December 13 - were informed of the decision via an e-mail.

'We are writing to inform you of the difficult decision to cancel our World Cup pre-camp and our planned participation in the World Cup,' the letter stated.

'We are extremely proud of the preparation by our players and coaches to represent the US in the World Cup.

'Unfortunately, in light of current circumstances including the US State Department's Level 3 Travel Advisory for Qatar and related directives for Embassy staff and families, we are no longer able to participate in the tournament. The well-being of our players and staff will always be our top priority.'

The United States has told players they will not participate in the FIFA U-17 World Cup in Qatar

The US State Department has a Level 3 travel advisory for the country amid ongoing conflicts

The USA won their CONCACAF qualifying group in order to reach the tournament

This announcement comes one week after coach Alex Aldaz announced his roster for the competition. The team was set to take part in a training camp this week at US Soccer's new headquarters outside Atlanta.

Players were set to open their tournament on November 21 with a match against Montenegro. They were also scheduled to face Chile and Algeria.

The United States earned the right to compete in the tournament by winning their group and outlasting the Dominican Republic on goal difference in CONCACAF U-17 World Cup qualifying.

The Baby Nats drew the Dominicans after beating Saint Kitts and Nevis as well as Saint Vincent and the Grenadines.

At last year's tournament, the Americans reached the Round of 32 before being eliminated by Morocco.

The best finish for the United states at the tournament came in New Zealand in 1999, when they came fourth - losing to Australia on penalties. Landon Donovan was named as the competition's Best Player.

FIFA made changes to the tournament last year, making it an annual event rather than once every two years. Qatar won the rights to host it from 2025 to 2029.

It is not clear which team will replace the United States at the tournament. The Daily Mail has contacted FIFA for comment on the matter.

#2146. Watford’s midfield struggles will continue until reinforcements are signed – here’s three January targets they must consider

A major issue for Watford this campaign has been the midfield. The summer departure of Imran Louza was a major blow, with his technical qualities, set-piece delivery and ability to dictate games sorely missed.

Similarly, Giorgi Chakvetadze’s transfer to Udinese has left the Hornets devoid of a creative spark, with his tendency to drift centrally and unlock defences. The club have simply not recruited adequate replacements, and they are suffering for it.

The current midfield options at Vicarage Road consist of Hector Kyprianou, Edo Kayembe, Edoardo Bove, and recent signing Martin Payero. None of these options are particularly offensive or astute going forward, lacking the ball-carrying and forward-thinking impetus of Chakvetadze, and the game-changing potential of Louza.

In order for Watford to change their fortunes this season, the club’s hierarchy need to address the glaring issues in midfield. Here, we put forward three targets the Hornets should consider moving for ahead of the January transfer window.

Patrick Kelly had a breakout season for Barnsley in 2025/26, establishing himself as one of League One’s most exciting prospects. He was touted for a potential step up, but stayed put and has remained a standout at Oakwell.

A busy and energetic player, Kelly could well offer Watford the spark they need in midfield. His excellent dribbling and physical attributes would complement a side in need of inspiration and dynamism. He ranks among the top 10% of players in his position League One for dribbles completed (FotMob).

Barnsley have had a disastrous start to the current campaign, and as such, a cut-price deal for the 22 year old could tempt clubs further up the footballing ladder.

Bate has been a critical part of highflying Stockport County’s success this season. The diminutive playmaker has contributed four assists and started all but one of the Hatters’ league games this campaign. He excels with his technical ability, ball retention and progression, and eye for a pass.

Having put behind him his injury issues from last season, he appears to have recaptured his impressive form from the 2024/45 campaign, cementing himself as a potential coup for alert Championship sides.

At 24 years of age, he represents a good blend of experience and potential to improve further. His position as club captain further demonstrates the leadership qualities necessary to navigate a Championship relegation scrap, which may await at Watford.

Hull City’s promotion and busy summer window has left players such as Kieran Dowell with limited minutes, opening the door to a potential exit. Dowell has been omitted from the Tigers’ Premier League squad, and the prospect of a January exit appears increasingly likely.

Dowell has donned the colours of six different Championship clubs, and has developed a reputation for scoring spectacular goals, as well as being an apt creator and technician. With three promotions at this level, he has the necessary pedigree and experience.

Out of contract in the summer, Dowell would represent a cost-effective option on either a short-term deal or a loan. His lack of playing time in recent seasons is a potential concern, but should he regain match fitness quickly he could be a useful addition to a threadbare Watford midfield.

As Watford seek to steer clear of the Championship’s lower reaches, bolstering the midfield is of high importance. The only summer addition in this area, Martin Payero, has been underwhelming when given the opportunity.

The fitness issues plaguing Edoardo Bove, the most talented player of the current options, has further stunted Watford’s progress.

The January window offers the club a chance to make amends for poor summer transfer business and to provide the squad with the best opportunity to consolidate their Championship status. The three midfield options put forward would help to push the club in the right direction.

#2145. “Important to take time” – Gabbia admits January return is possible with right precautions

Matteo Gabbia has admitted that he could return to the field in January for AC Milan, but he doesn’t want to rush back.

It has been a difficult season for Milan, at least where injuries are concerned. In defence, they have had issues reminiscent of the 2023/24 season, where Gabbia had to be brought back from his loan spell at Villarreal.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

This season, though, it seems Gerry Cardinale is going to go down a different route and spend to help Ruben Amorim with his defensive options. Indeed, there have been plenty of rumours about recruitments at the back.

However, Gabbia could again be a key returnee in January, with him hoping to return from injury early in the new year.

Speaking to Sky Sport (via MilanNews) – at an event organised by the Milan Foundation at Milan’s “Arturo Toscanini” secondary school, where they met with several children with disabilities – Gabbia discussed the importance of the event, as well as his recovery.

“It’s wonderful, very enjoyable to spend hours with them. It’s also beautiful to see how they manage to create a group, something that really shows the importance of teamwork, the importance of friendship, even among themselves.

“We’re happy; we thank Fondazione Milan for the project, which is always beautiful and important: we’re happy to be a part of it and we’re enjoying the afternoon.”

Are you able to rejoin the group in January?

“Yes, that could be the time: we’re trying to work hard and do everything as best we can. I obviously have to thank the physiotherapists and the doctor because we’re really doing everything we can every day to try to heal the injury and get back on the pitch feeling good.

“It’s definitely been an unlucky season for me so far: I don’t want it to happen again or for any recurrences, so it’s also important to take the right time, get back in shape, and support the team right now.”

“Although it’s bad for the team that there are so many injured players, we’re together right now, supporting each other and trying to encourage each other through therapy, gym work, and the entire recovery process. Therefore, we’re trying to work as a team this week too, to overcome all the players’ injuries.

“From a personal standpoint, I’m trying to take this time calmly, in the sense that these things are part of sport, part of life, and not all moments are the same.

“This is certainly a difficult time, but it still allows me to stay close to the team, to try to lend my support, even in the good and bad times. That’s what I’m doing, in addition to obviously doing what I can to try to return as soon as possible and make my contribution on the pitch.”

How do you explain the team’s ups and downs?

“I think it’s something we need to get our heads around; we need to understand the importance of every game.

“The other day I heard our coach give an interview in which he said something important: we need to understand that every game, when you don’t win, is taken as a defeat, a heavy defeat. We need to truly understand the importance of every game and approach training to prepare 100%, as best as possible, for every match.

“Therefore, I think this is room for improvement, but I see a group that is really eager to improve, to work, and as a result, I’m sure we’ll be able to fix this flaw as well.

“Although we’ve had a good start to the year in the league and less so in the Europa League, we have plenty of time to improve and have a strong year.”

#2144. 3 strikers West Ham should target in January as Scott Parker sets new talisman demand

West Ham have the busy December schedule to see to before the January transfer window comes around, but Scott Parker is already intent on improving his squad. And, he’s identified two areas where he wants to sign new players: striker and left-back.

The centre-forward has been a position of contention for WHUFC, despite the fact they are the league’s top scorers with a remarkable 37 goals in 15 games. None of Joel Piroe, Pablo or Taty Castellanos have proven they can find consistent goalscoring form, and the latter reportedly wants to leave the London Stadium.

With that in mind, we put forward three strikers West Ham should target to resolve their dilemma.

Parker is said to prefer Championship experience, so who better than someone who is already starring in the division? Andri Gudjohnsen has been in scintillating form for Blackburn Rovers, with a goals per shot rate of 0.50 ranking him among the most clinical finishers in Europe this season.

The 24-year-old has scored 11 goals in 15 games this term and has starred on the international stage for Iceland too. Gudjohnsen scored four in two games over the November break.

A fast and powerful forward with smart movement and finishing, Gudjohnsen could come in and lead the line for West Ham and he’d be backed to make the step up to the Premier League too. It would not be a cheap signing, but he could be just what Parker needs.

Looking slightly further afield, one man who could catch the Hammers’ eye is Paulos Abraham. The 24-year-old was highly rated in his youth but a spell at Dutch side FC Groningen didn’t go as hoped, resulting in his return to Sweden with Hammarby IF.

Since returning to the Allsvenskan in January 2025, he has been one of the league’s most dangerous forwards. 17 goals in 28 league games this season take Abraham to 34 goals in 74 outings for the club, and he was rewarded with his first Sweden call-up earlier this season.

Abraham would bring electric pace to West Ham’s frontline, and his inventive finishing sees him score from unlikely situations. His ability to run beyond Championship defences could bring a new threat to a highly creative WHUFC outfit.

Last but not least is certainly the most ambitious of the lot. However, Aleksandar Mitrovic has had a slightly disrupted recent spell in the Middle East due to a ligament injury and may be tempted to return to London under the lead of Parker again.

The Serbian powerhouse played and starred under the new West Ham boss at Fulham, and he would be a dream addition for Parker. Mitrovic is one of the most prolific forwards in Championship history with 85 goals in 126 games at the level and he would certainly fit the bill as a prolific scorer who can lead the Hammers to promotion.

The main obstacle is obvious though. Mitrovic is on massive money in Qatar with Al-Rayyan, and even if WHUFC can persuade the club to let him leave, the 32-year-old might not be minded to leave his sizeable contract.

But, if the opportunity was there, Mitrovic would be a perfect solution for Parker’s striker issue.

Published: 21:52 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 21:57 EST, 11 November 2026

A reported record-breaking pay offer and the chance to live closer to Nathan Cleary were not enough to bring Mary Fowler back to Australia.

The proposal could have offered a solution to the long-distance relationship that keeps their careers on opposite sides of the world.

For battling NRL franchise Wests Tigers, it was an extraordinary bid to prise one of rugby league’s biggest names away from Penrith.

According to News Corp, Tigers owner Holman Barnes Group proposed a combined package worth $9million, including a $1million marquee contract for Fowler to join the Central Coast Mariners women’s team.

The group had acquired the Mariners women’s side, opening the door to a recruitment pitch spanning two sports.

Mary Fowler remains at Manchester City despite a reported $1million offer to return to Australian football

A proposed recruitment package would have brought Fowler to the Central Coast Mariners and Cleary to Wests Tigers

Fowler returned to Australia in October to support Cleary following two operations

Under the reported proposal, Cleary would have joined the Tigers and Fowler would have returned to NSW on what would be the highest salary in A-League Women history.

But the blockbuster homecoming did not materialise.

Fowler, 23, is contracted to Manchester City until 2028, meaning any move would also require an agreement with the English club over her release.

She has built a formidable profile away from the pitch, with News Corp reporting that her multi-year Adidas deal is worth $10million.

Her other commercial work includes a L’Oréal Paris ambassadorship and Chobani’s national ‘Game Changer’ campaign.

The couple’s long-distance relationship attracted renewed attention in October when Fowler returned to Australia to support Cleary as he recovered from two operations.

Fowler’s commercial pulling power extends well beyond football, with  her multi-year Adidas deal worth $10million

Fowler became L’Oréal Paris’s first female athlete ambassador for Australia and New Zealand in 2024, adding a major beauty partnership to her growing profile

Fowler fronts Chobani’s national ‘Game Changer’ campaign, appearing across billboards, public transport and digital advertising as the brand launches its high-protein drinks

Should athletes choose love or loyalty to their teams when faced with life-changing offers?

Cleary had fractured his fibula and dislocated his ankle during Penrith’s preliminary final loss to Newcastle, with a bone breaking through the skin.

He also had a fracture in his other foot.

Panthers chairman Greg Alexander later explained that the damage, while confronting, could have been worse.

‘The tibia didn’t break, no fracture in the tibia, which was fantastic news,’ Alexander told SEN.

#2140. Belgian journalist admits Sylla may be ‘more inclined’ to move to England: “His dream destination”

AC Milan have been heavily interested in Massire Sylla recently, but it seems they may struggle to sign him, let alone in January.

It’s no secret that Milan want to sign a central defender and whilst Goncalo Inacio is the leading name, he’s not the only one in the mix. Instead, there are several public names of interest and Sylla is one of them.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

First linked by Matteo Moretto, his name has become more popular in reports linking the Rossoneri to defenders and he seems like a concrete target for the January mercato. Yet, it seems like it will be difficult to bring him to San Siro.

Speaking to MilanNews, Het Nieuwsblad journalist Tjorven Messiaen looked at Massire Sylla and what his future had in store.

In Italy, there’s been talk of AC Milan’s interest in Sylla. What’s being said about him in Belgium?

“I saw that AC Milan have linked him with him. In Belgium, we discovered him through social media, and the USG hasn’t confirmed anything yet. It’s possible, however, that AC Milan has drawn attention to the boy, because he’s playing great football.

“His move to AC Milan is unlikely to materialise in January, however, as both Sylla and the club would be more inclined to listen to offers from the Premier League. He’s the kind of player who would be a perfect fit for the English league.”

What are his main strengths and best qualities?

“He’s young, he’s good in duels, he’s quick. He’s also very good at ball control. He’s a complete defender; he has the potential to become one of the best, and he’d be a perfect fit in the Premier League, which, among other things, is Sylla’s dream destination.”

What aspects of his game still need improvement?

“It’s hard to say, especially since his growth in recent months has been impressive. I’d say he needs to gain more experience, which will come simply by playing. So I don’t see many things he needs to improve to take the next step in his career.”

Do you think Union Saint-Gilloise (USG) would be willing to let him leave in January, or do you think any transfer discussions will be postponed until the summer?

“I don’t think USG will let him leave in January. The only possible scenario, which I don’t think will happen, but which is the only solution anyway, is for USG to sign him in January but keep him at least until the end of the season, especially since the club never sells its best players in January.

“I also think interested clubs could spend a lot of money, but Union won’t let him leave in January.”

Could you describe him in three adjectives?

“Quick, physical, and full of character, because he’s someone who always has problems when he has to make decisions.”

“Considering his development, I’d say Pacho. He came from nowhere in Belgium, developed, moved to Frankfurt, and then ended up at PSG. In Sylla’s case, I think he’ll go straight to the Premier League. He has everything it takes to be perfect for this league.”

He’s still very young. Do you think he’s ready to play for a big club in a big city like Milan?

“He’s still very young, but he’s humble, calm, and we mustn’t forget that he arrived last year, in January, from the Norwegian second division. He played very little last year, but he’s very strong mentally and has started this season as if he’s been playing with this team for years.

“I can’t imagine USG without Sylla today, especially since we’re talking about one of the best defenders in the league. At the end of the season, he’ll make a significant leap in his career, but in my opinion it won’t be AC ​​Milan because he’s waiting for the Premier League. And from what I know, several English clubs are already ready to make him a player.

“So AC Milan could give him a try, but it will require a big project and a lot of money.”

We shall have to see how January develops and whether he could be sold on the project. However, it certainly doesn’t bode well from these comments.

#2139. How Michael Carrick's Man United fell apart: Players' bust-up on flight back from Madrid, weakness in training and on touchline - and why dressing-room sources believe divided board 'hung him out to dry'

By CHRIS WHEELER, NORTHERN SPORTS WRITER and NATHAN SALT - FOOTBALL REPORTER

Published: 10:30 EST, 11 November 2026 | Updated: 06:44 EST, 12 November 2026

For the last two days, Michael Carrick has followed his familiar route towards Manchester United’s training base, nudging his black Land Rover past the security barrier at Carrington before 8am, and making his way into work just as he has for much of the last two decades as a player, coach and head coach at the club.

While Carrick's fate has been decided by men in suits in Monaco and Manchester, he has put on his training gear and gone through the motions of coaching whichever players are left during the international break.

It has become increasingly apparent that Carrick was a dead man walking in the two weeks since the United board gave him a stay of execution and bought themselves time after the 3-0 defeat by Bournemouth last month.

Having effectively been brought in as a caretaker when United plucked him off the beach in Barbados in mid-January, he left much the same way: merely keeping the seat warm for Mauricio Pochettino in the chaotic days that have led to his sacking.

Sources close to the United dressing room believe Carrick has been ‘hung out to dry’ as United dithered over his future and then began a futile attempt to appoint Pochettino as his replacement in secret.

This is the story of how Carrick's United fell apart. It is the story of how disgruntled players aired their dirty laundry in public and clashed in private. It is the story of how the club's boardroom became the backdrop for an executive power struggle. And it is the story of how the magic touch that saw Carrick land the job permanently just five months ago deserted him...

Michael Carrick is out as Manchester United manager - just ten league games after guiding United to third and being handed a two-year contract

Carrick has had to turn up as normal at Carrington this week even as the world knew he was for the chop. Some dressing-room sources say he has been 'hung out to dry'

Carrick would have seen the writing on the wall when he got up to give an impressive speech at a dinner in the Heritage Suite at Old Trafford on Friday night to celebrate the 40th anniversary of Sir Alex Ferguson’s appointment.

The significance of the occasion was not lost on Carrick. Ferguson was brought in to succeed Ron Atkinson on the same date in November 1986 with United one place above the relegation zone. Having boxed himself into an identical corner, Carrick too was on borrowed time.

Two days later, he stood helplessly on the touchline at Old Trafford as United played out a ridiculous 4-4 draw with Aston Villa. Twice his team fought back from two goals down, but it was the final proof for United bosses that change was unavoidable.

The biggest club in English football having to endure the humiliation of at least two weeks in the relegation zone was too much for them to stomach.

His body language after the Bournemouth game when a tearful and crestfallen Carrick offered what looked like a farewell wave to the Stretford End – four days after he approached fans in Como to apologise for an equally painful 4-1 Champions League surrender – suggested he was already done.

The 45-year-old limped on to another defeat at Chelsea, then presided over a barmy 3-2 comeback win over Roma and the bonkers draw with Villa. A consummate and composed midfielder for United, Carrick of all people knew this wasn’t the way football should be played.

All the while, he spoke like a man who could turn things around. They always do. But the performances and results told a different story.

His critics would argue that someone whose only previous job in management was at Middlesbrough should never have been put in charge of United in the first place.

Others would say the bravest move by the club would have been to thank Carrick for his admirable efforts in securing third place and Champions League qualification last season, and move on in the summer when Pochettino would have been available for free before signing a new contract as US coach with a £7million release clause.

Matheus Cunha's late winner sealed a remarkable comeback win over Roma last week - but it promised to be a false dawn for Carrick

The 45-year-old's body language after an earlier loss to Bournemouth had suggested he was done back then, with Carrick appearing to wave farewell to the Stretford End

Instead, Carrick was rewarded with a two-year deal in May and the wheels fell off in spectacular fashion. Rather like his friend and former team-mate Ole Gunnar Solskjaer, the honeymoon period gave way to something altogether less appealing.

At least Solskjaer stuck around for three more years. Having beaten the Norwegian to the job in January, Carrick’s reign lasted less than 10 months.

The similarities between the two men are striking. Ex-players who basked in the glory days under Ferguson and know what it takes to succeed at Old Trafford, but were incapable of lifting the modern-day Manchester United to similar heights.

Having replaced Ruben Amorim and inspired a remarkable turnaround in the second half of last season, Carrick must have thought he had a chance.

His calm demeanour and experience of life in the Old Trafford pressure cooker that had played a part in his appointment was in contrast to the emotional incontinence of Amorim. How strange then that it has given way to such calamity and chaos.

United got off to the worst possible start by losing at newly-promoted Hull City in August. Tigers boss Sergej Jakirovic belittled them by saying his goalkeeper Konstantinos Tzolakis might as well have been sipping a cup of tea, and the hits have just kept on coming for Carrick.

Aside from Bournemouth, there were the late equalisers scored by Ainsley Maitland-Niles and Dominic Solanke for Everton and Tottenham, the controversial winner conceded to 10-man Manchester City, crashing out of the Carabao Cup to Brighton after giving up a two-goal lead, and the defeats in Europe by Atletico Madrid and Como after going behind in the opening seconds.

By the time Alysson scored in the first minute for Villa on Sunday, United’s defence had become as leaky as the Old Trafford roof. The four goals Carrick’s side conceded took it to 15 in his final four games.

United's defence has been leaky all season long, with Ainsley Maitland-Niles' stoppage-time equaliser at Hill Dickinson Stadium among the goals to cost them valuable points

By the time Alysson scored in the first minute for Aston Villa on Sunday, United’s defence had become as leaky as the Old Trafford roof. They let in 15 in his final four games

So why the alarming decline from last season? The United players haven’t looked sharp after a disjointed World Cup summer that involved a more relaxed one-week training camp in Ireland. There have been too many injuries.

Some players have spoken of a drop-off in training under Carrick and his backroom staff. Others feel that the switch to a more possession-based 4-2-4 system was asking too much of a group who aren’t suited to it.

There was surprise that a man so invested in the club's academy used so few young players. Harry Amass was left kicking his heels despite the chronic shortage of left backs, JJ Gabriel's shock exit was an unwelcome distraction, and Shea Lacey's re-emergence came too late.

It would be grossly unfair to pin this all on Carrick though. He is just the latest unfortunate to pay the price for the shambles at United which runs from the boardroom to the dressing room.

Amid ongoing uncertainty over his future, there has been a power struggle behind the scenes between director of football Jason Wilcox and director of recruitment Christopher Vivell over who to replace him, while sources close to the Glazer family have revealed a growing dissatisfaction at how minority owner Sir Jim Ratcliffe and Ineos are running the club.

United failed Carrick in the transfer market last summer with a futile pursuit of his No 1 midfield target Elliot Anderson, and instead delivered Youri Tielemans and Andrey Santos.

Carrick could see trouble on the horizon when he spoke to the media at Carton House in Maynooth during that pre-season camp in early August, but tried not to rock the boat.

It was the day his daughter Louise was getting her A-level results. ‘You know, we would like more, we need to keep pushing for more,’ he said, using the word ‘push’ another nine times.

There has been a power struggle behind the scenes between director of football Jason Wilcox (centre) and director of recruitment Christopher Vivell (left) over who to replace Carrick

Some players have spoken of a drop-off in training under Carrick and his backroom staff

Carlos Baleba followed but an ankle injury meant that he couldn’t play until October, and the £70m signing then suffered a hamstring injury.

United blundered catastrophically by focusing on midfield and delaying improvements to other areas of the team until next summer. In particular, the decision not to sign cover for Luke Shaw and Benjamin Sesko backfired spectacularly as both players succumbed to injury.

Lisandro Martinez called out United’s underwhelming recruitment in Madrid by saying he expected more of Wilcox, and he isn’t the only one who feels that way.

Tielemans gave a terse ‘no comment’ when he was asked on camera about his team-mate’s remarks, but it’s understood the two players clashed on the team flight back to Manchester as cracks began to show in an increasingly unhappy camp.

The camaraderie fostered by rounds of golf involving Carrick and the players during the break in Ireland was beginning to fall apart.

It came to a head when captain Bruno Fernandes became the second member of the leadership group to speak out of turn, questioning Carrick’s tactics after the loss to Como.

Having continuously defended his players in the face of criticism from some of his former United team-mates over their lack of effort, Carrick’s spiky response that ‘if you don't run forward and you don't run back, you aren’t going to win’ perhaps revealed his true feelings about this group.

He and Fernandes settled their differences in a clear-the-air meeting, but the questions didn’t go away. When he was asked about their relationship again at the end of what proved to be his final pre-match press conference last Friday, Carrick tapped his inquisitor on the shoulder as he left the room at Carrington and said with a smile: ‘Nice try!’

It was typical of Carrick, who often met questions with the knowing grin of a man who couldn’t say what he really thought.

Lisandro Martinez and Youri Tielemans clashed on United's flight back to Manchester following the former's incendiary comments after a loss to Atletico Madrid

One issue has been Carrick’s rather passionless appearance on the touchline. ‘He doesn't upset anyone, but doesn't inspire anyone either,’ remarked one dressing room source

It got to the point where he was encouraged by the club to be a little more forthcoming in press conferences. There have been some barbed post-match exchanges in TV interviews with Sky Sports and Viaplay this season, and the five-minute monologue before the Bournemouth game was pure theatre.

But on the whole, Carrick’s ability to play a straight bat in front of the cameras was a welcome change for the Old Trafford hierarchy compared to the loose-lipped Amorim – particularly with the Amazon Prime documentary-makers following their every move.

‘He talks a lot but doesn't say much,’ said Roy Keane, never Carrick’s biggest fan, on Sunday. ‘I don't know if that's a tactic, like a politician.’

More of an issue was Carrick’s rather passionless appearance on the touchline, which seemed to be reflected in his team at times.

Always dressed in black, a hand placed studiously on his chin, it has contrasted with more animated adversaries such as Unai Emery, Roberto De Zerbi and Diego Simeone in recent weeks.

‘He doesn't upset anyone, but doesn't inspire anyone either,’ remarked one dressing room source.

The fans were always behind him though, even the ones who may have accepted some time ago that a change was needed. Support and sympathy were never in short supply for a coach who has been let down by the men in the directors’ box and the players on the pitch.

That much has been abundantly clear by the abuse meted out to Ratcliffe and Co this season, and the unprecedented scenes when the fans turned on the players after Bournemouth.

Carrick looks set to be replaced by former Tottenham and Chelsea boss Mauricio Pochettino (right)

Carrick has been known to stand alongside them in the past, and it would be no surprise to see him there again in future.

‘Oh, it’s Carrick you know…’ echoed his tribute song from the Stretford End as he disappeared down the tunnel for the last time on Sunday evening.

So another bites the dust. A good man. A United man. The right man for the job? Maybe not, as it turns out. That poisoned chalice now passes to Mauricio Pochettino.

#2138. Manchester United defence: Why Carrick must trust Yoro and Heaven

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

Manchester United endured another embarrassing draw against 10-man Tottenham after failing to register their second win despite bringing in a plethora of players.

Manchester United invested heavily last season in their attacking options, bringing in Bryan Mbeumo and Matheus Cunha.

This season, they overhauled their midfield. United spent massively on Carlos Baleba, Youri Tielemans and Andrey Santos. Despite spending massively, they have failed to produce the control they would have expected in the midfield. The lack of control was evident in the recent Tottenham game, where Mainoo-Tielemans partnership failed to control the game collectively.

The midfield question was covered in our recent report as well, highlighting a lack of control and carried comments from Gary Neville and Jamie Redknapp.

The bigger question is when United will strengthen their defence. As per our previous report as well, Manchester United are planning to overhaul their defence in the upcoming transfer windows.

United were heavily linked with several left backs but eventually failed to bring any one. The situation left Luke Shaw as the only senior option in the position, while Patrick Dorgu is used upfront by Michael Carrick.

Harry Amass is another option for Michael Carrick, but he is only here because of a failed transfer move. He has been rarely used at left back as Dalot and Mazraoui get the nod in the position ahead of him.

The situation at right-back isn’t all good either. Diogo Dalot is the first option there, but he rarely provides the assurance that puts Michael Carrick at ease. Noussair Mazraoui is the second choice at right-back, but his attacking numbers aren’t great either.

Harry Maguire and Lisandro Martinez form up United’s centre-back pairing while Leny Yoro and Ayden Heaven are used all over the defensive line in rotation when needed. Yoro was used at right-back against Tottenham while Heaven arrived as left-back towards the end of the game.

Manchester United’s season hasn’t been defensively strong or anywhere near being good. They have conceded goals in all Premier League games and only kept one clean sheet against Sabah in the Champions League.

Lisandro Martinez has come under scrutiny after errors on four occasions. Against Ipswich Town, Manchester United won 5-2, but his error resulted in Ipswich’s second goal.

His own goal against Fulham gave away two points at Craven Cottage in the 1-1 draw. Another error led to Solanke’s goal against Tottenham, when he took his eyes off the ball.

His injury concerns are another aspect, and maybe Michael Carrick needs to make a decision.

Manchester United’s success depended heavily on youth over the years. When pundits questioned them, Sir Alex Ferguson gave them the chance to prove them wrong. Alan Hansen made the famous quote “You can’t win anything with kids” on the BBC’s Match of the Day on August 19, 1995. United did exactly that, played youth and went on to win everything.

Michael Carrick needs to do exactly that. He needs to trust youth and allow them to make mistakes. Leny Yoro and Ayden Heaven arrived as massive potentials, but they haven’t been given the license to express themselves, without fearing for their places. Both the youngsters have rarely started together or in a stretch of games. It is maybe time for them to take their chances.

Michael Carrick needs to keep Harry Maguire as a senior experienced figure around them. There is no doubt over Martinez’s ability, but he is not the answer to United’s long-term problem. United need to blend youth with experience as their identity moving forward. Lisandro Martinez perhaps isn’t the answer to United’s problem.

A defensive revival expected in the upcoming transfer windows needs to happen now. It must happen with chances to the Youth. Michael Carrick needs to be brave enough and throw the youngsters in the mix. What it will bring is a long-term solution. They need to experience the errors and pressures that will shape them in football.

#2133. Why must Manchester United move beyond the Glazers?

Shumail Sajid writes about Manchester United for Read Man Utd and Crystal Palace for Read Crystal Palace, with occasional pieces on other Premier League clubs. He has also written for FanSided's Red Devils Dispatch, Yardbarker, Anfield Central and The Sports Side.

The Glazers took control of Manchester United in 2005. That takeover has since caused so many disruptions and protests by the fans over massive debts and insufficient backing of the team in the transfer market, despite successful periods in the Premier League and Europe.

Now Manchester United need to think about who should control the club in the future. Laurie Whitwell has now shared his views on why Manchester United need an ownership change. He simply wants the Glazers gone from Old Trafford. However, he also recognizes that replacing the current ownership doesn’t automatically mean things will improve for United.

The journalist believes the change in ownership has had a lasting effect on United, with the club still feeling its consequences today. A fresh start might be welcome, but the identity of the next owners would matter just as much as the departure of the existing ones.

“Personally, I would like to see the Glazers gone from Manchester United,” Laurie Whitwell said on The Rest is Football podcast with Gary Lineker.

“That change, you know, just really affected and still impacts United now.”

The desire for different ownership is one thing, but finding people capable of taking the club forward is another.

Whitwell admitted there is no perfect answer when it comes to who should take charge.

That leaves United facing a difficult choice. A sale could offer the chance to change how the club is run, but simply bringing in a new owner would not guarantee better decisions or a brighter future.

Whitwell also raised the idea of United supporters having a greater say in the club’s ownership and decisions, much like the Barcelona model where fans own the club itself. However, he highlighted that the Barcelona model isn’t ideal either and has its own shortcomings.

Changing the names at the top would only be one part of the change. The club would also need an ownership structure capable of making sound decisions and providing a direction.

The issue of ownership goes beyond whether the Glazers should stay or leave. Manchester United needs to find the right people and the right model to secure the club’s future. Getting that decision wrong could leave them facing the same circus the fans are enduring under the Glazers.
